C,C_{s},C_{f}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{c_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{I}}\otimes(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}})^{\otimes k_{A}}\otimes(\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}})^{\otimes k_{B}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}})
. These operators are characterised by

\displaystyle C_{s}

\displaystyle\geq 0

(40)

\displaystyle C_{f}=C-C_{s}

\displaystyle\geq 0

(41)

\displaystyle\text{tr}(C)

\displaystyle=d_{c_{I}}d_{t_{I}}d_{A_{O}}^{k_{A}}d_{B_{O}}^{k_{B}}

(42)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{k}(C),

(43)

where
k=k_{A}+k_{B}
is the total number of slots in the quantum comb
C
, and
\mathbb{P}_{k}
is the projector onto the subspace spanned by
k
-slot quantum combs.

The projector
\mathbb{P}_{k}
is defined in full generality in Ref.
[
5
]
. For sake of completeness, we explicitly write
\mathbb{P}_{k}
here in the cases where
k\in\{2,3,4\}
, which are the ones involved in our numerical calculations. To simplify the notation in the definition of the projector, let us define a
k
-slot quantum comb by its Choi operator
C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{F})
. Then, let us define the trace-and-replace operation acting on the subspace
\mathcal{H}^{X}
of an operator
C
as

{}_{X}C\coloneqq\text{tr}_{X}(C)\otimes\frac{\mathbb{1}^{C}}{d_{C}}.

(44)

We are now ready to explicitly write the projector
\mathbb{P}_{k}
for
k\in\{2,3,4\}
. For the case where
k=2
,

\mathbb{P}_{2}(C)=\,C-\,_{F}C+\,_{O_{2}F}C-\,_{I_{2}O_{2}F}C+\,_{O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F}C-\,_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F}C+\,_{PI_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F}C.

(45)

For the case where
k=3
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{3}(C)=&\ C-\,_{F}C+\,_{O_{3}F}C-\,_{I_{3}O_{3}F}C+\,_{O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}C-\,_{I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}C+\,_{O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}C\\
&-\,_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}C+\,_{PI_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}C.\end{split}

(46)

Finally, for the case where
k=4
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{4}(C)=&\ C-\,_{F}C+\,_{O_{4}F}C-\,_{I_{4}O_{4}F}C+\,_{O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C-\,_{I_{3}O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C+\,_{O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C-\,_{I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C\\
&+\,_{O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C-\,_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C+\,_{PI_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}I_{4}O_{4}F}C.\end{split}

(47)

For convenience, we also define the dual affine projector used in the dual formulation of the SDP in the main text [Eq. (
28
)]. Given a set of operators
\mathcal{A}
, its dual affine set
\mathcal{B}
is the set of all operators
B
such that
\text{tr}(A^{\dagger}B)=1
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
. Hence, the dual affine set of the set of Choi operators of quantum combs
C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{F})
is the set of all operators
\Gamma\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{P}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{F})
such that
\text{tr}(C\,\Gamma)=1
. The operators in the dual affine set of quantum combs are themselves a particular case of quantum combs, and can be characterized by projectors
\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{k}
given by
[
5
]

\overline{\mathbb{P}}_{k}(\Gamma)=\Gamma-\mathbb{P}_{k}(\Gamma)+\,_{PI_{1}I_{2}\ldots I_{k}O_{k}F}\Gamma.

(48)

In our numerical calculations, we used the code available in the repository of Ref.
[
5
]
to generate the above projector constraints.

A3
QC-CCs and probabilistic QC-CCs

We now present the explicit constraints for QC-CC transformations. QC-CCs and probabilistic QC-CCs have been defined in full generality and for any number of slots in Ref.
[
27
]
. Here, we write these definitions explicitly for the cases of
k=2
and
k=3
slots, which were used in our numerical calculations. Since we only evaluated the maximal probability of simulating the quantum switch with a QC-CC in the restricted simulation scenario, where the input control and target systems are fixed, we write the explicit constraints for a probabilistic QC-CC with fixed input systems (compared to the definition of quantum combs in this section, this is the equivalent of a scenario where
d_{P}=1
).

Unlike the case of quantum combs, to define the Choi operator
W=W_{s}+W_{f}
associated to a probabilistic QC-CC, where
W,W_{s},W_{f}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{I_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{1}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}^{I_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{O_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{F})
, it is necessary but not sufficient to say that
W_{s}\geq 0
,
W_{f}=W-W_{s}\geq 0
and
W
is a QC-CC, as individual constraints must be applied to
W_{s}
and
W_{f}
as well to ensure validity of the overall transformation.

In the case where
k=2
, we have that

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W_{s}+W_{f}

(49)

\displaystyle\text{tr}(W)

\displaystyle=d_{O_{1}}d_{O_{2}},

(50)

where

W_{s}=W_{s}^{12F}+W_{s}^{21F},\hskip 28.45274ptW_{f}=W_{f}^{12F}+W_{f}^{21F},

(51)

such that
W_{s}^{12F}\geq 0
,
W_{s}^{21F}\geq 0
,
W_{f}^{12F}\geq 0
, and
W_{f}^{21F}\geq 0
, and additionally
W^{12F}\coloneqq W_{s}^{12F}+W_{f}^{12F}
must be a quantum comb with the order
I_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F
, and
W^{21F}\coloneqq W_{s}^{21F}+W_{f}^{21F}
must be a quantum comb with the order
I_{2}O_{2}I_{1}O_{1}F
.

In the case where
k=3
, we have that

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W_{s}+W_{f}

(52)

\displaystyle\text{tr}(W)

\displaystyle=d_{O_{1}}d_{O_{2}}d_{O_{3}}.

(53)

where

\displaystyle W_{s}

\displaystyle=W_{s}^{123F}+W_{s}^{132F}+W_{s}^{213F}+W_{s}^{231F}+W_{s}^{312F}+W_{s}^{321F}

(54)

\displaystyle W_{f}

\displaystyle=W_{f}^{123F}+W_{f}^{132F}+W_{f}^{213F}+W_{f}^{231F}+W_{f}^{312F}+W_{f}^{321F},

(55)

where
W_{s}^{xyzF}\geq 0
and
W_{f}^{xyzF}\geq 0
for all
xyz\in\mathrm{Perm}(1,2,3)
. Moreover, we define
W^{xyzF}\coloneqq W_{s}^{xyzF}+W_{f}^{xyzF}
for all
xyz\in\mathrm{Perm}(1,2,3)
and impose that the operators
W^{123F}
and
W^{132F}
must satisfy

{}_{F}W^{123F}=\,_{O_{3}F}W^{123F},\hskip 17.07164pt

\hskip 17.07164pt\hskip 17.07164pt{}_{F}W^{132F}=\,_{O_{2}F}W^{132F}

(56)

{}_{I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{123F}=\,_{O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{123F},

\hskip 17.07164pt{}_{I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{132F}=\,_{O_{3}I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{132F}

(57)

{}_{I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{123F}+_{I_{3}O_{3}I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{132F}

\displaystyle=\,_{O_{1}}(_{I_{2}O_{2}I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{123F}+_{I_{3}O_{3}I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{132F}).

(58)

Similarly,
W^{213F}
and
W^{231F}
must satisfy

{}_{F}W^{213F}=\,_{O_{3}F}W^{213F},\hskip 17.07164pt

\hskip 17.07164pt\hskip 17.07164pt{}_{F}W^{231F}=\,_{O_{1}F}W^{231F}

(59)

{}_{I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{213F}=\,_{O_{1}I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{213F},

\hskip 17.07164pt{}_{I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{231F}=\,_{O_{3}I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{231F}

(60)

{}_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{213F}+_{I_{3}O_{3}I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{231F}

\displaystyle=\,_{O_{2}}(_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{3}O_{3}F}W^{213F}+_{I_{3}O_{3}I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{231F}).

(61)

Finally,
W^{312F}
and
W^{321F}
must satisfy

{}_{F}W^{312F}=\,_{O_{2}F}W^{312F},\hskip 17.07164pt

\hskip 17.07164pt\hskip 17.07164pt{}_{F}W^{321F}=\,_{O_{1}F}W^{321F}

(62)

{}_{I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{312F}=\,_{O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{312F},

\hskip 17.07164pt{}_{I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{321F}=\,_{O_{2}I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{321F}

(63)

{}_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{312F}+_{I_{2}O_{2}I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{321F}

\displaystyle=\,_{O_{3}}(_{I_{1}O_{1}I_{2}O_{2}F}W^{312F}+_{I_{2}O_{2}I_{1}O_{1}F}W^{321F}).

(64)

Appendix B
General simulation scenarios

In this section, we discuss the differences between simulation scenarios where the quantum switch acts on general quantum channels, on only part of general quantum channels, or on quantum instruments, highlighting how they relate to the action of the quantum switch on unitary channels. We also demonstrate how the switch simulation that we introduced in the circuit in Eq. (
7
) related to Thm.
1
fits into this context.

B1
The action of the quantum switch on bipartite quantum channels

Figure B1:
General simulation of the quantum switch.
A higher-order transformation
\mathcal{C}
, which can be a quantum comb or a QC-CC, that acts on part of several copies of input bipartite quantum channels
A
and
B
is a simulation of the quantum switch
\mathcal{S}
if it reproduces the action of the quantum switch on all arbitrary pairs of bipartite channels
A
and
B
.

The first simulation scenario discussed in the main text is the one where the quantum switch
\mathcal{S}
acts on the “entire” quantum channels
A
and
B
, as represented in Fig. 2, in the main text. Formally, this is the case where the quantum switch
\mathcal{S}
acts on a pair of single-party (single input system, single output system) channels

A:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}})

and

B:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}})
,
and outputs another channel

\mathcal{S}(A,B):\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{c_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{O}})
.
However, quantum theory also allows one to apply the quantum switch on
parts
of bipartite (two input systems, two output systems) channels
A:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}})
and
B:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}})
, where the dimension of the primed spaces is arbitrary. This results in the quantum channel
\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}(A,B):\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{c_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}})
, where
\mathcal{I}
is the identity higher-order transformation. Since the dimension of the primed spaces is arbitrary, all multi-partite channels can be described in this context as bipartite channels, which are the most general kinds of deterministic transformations between quantum states one can consider. This case is illustrated in Fig. 3 of the main text.

As mentioned in the main text, the impossibility of simulating the action of the quantum switch on all single-party quantum channels, for some number of calls
k_{A}
and
k_{B}
, implies the impossibility of simulating the action of the quantum switch on all bipartite channels with the same number of calls. Hence, when focusing on no-go simulation proofs, we can restrict ourselves to considering only single-party channels. However, conversely, should a simulation of the action of the quantum switch on all single-party channels exist for some number of calls
k_{A}
and
k_{B}
, this does not necessarily imply that a simulation would also exists for the action of the quantum switch on all bipartite channels.

For this reason, in order to have a fully general simulation of the quantum switch, one must consider the case where the quantum switch acts on only part of a bipartite channel, as illustrated in Fig.
B1
. That is, a full simulation of the quantum switch is only obtained when, for every pair of bipartite channels
A:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}})

and

B:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}})
, there exists
k_{A},k_{B}\in\mathbb{N}
such that,

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes k_{A}},B^{\otimes k_{B}})=\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}(A,B)

(65)

where
\mathcal{C}
is a quantum comb, or a QC-CC. This most general simulation scenario is depicted in Fig.
B1
.

In the next section, we show how a simulation of the action of the quantum switch on all pairs of bipartite channels indeed covers all possible quantum operations the quantum switch could take as input, including the probabilistic ones, such as quantum instruments.

B2
The action of the quantum switch on quantum instruments

Higher-order transformations like the quantum switch can also be applied to quantum instruments, which describe the most general probabilistic transformation between quantum states.

Quantum instruments are transformations that map a quantum state to another quantum state with a certain probability, also outputting a classical outcome. Physically, this describes, for instance, a measurement process where both a classical outcome and a post-measurement quantum state are produced. They can be described by a collection of CP maps
\{I_{a}\}_{a}
which add to a CPTP map, i.e., such that
\sum_{a}I_{a}
is CPTP. A quantum instrument takes a quantum state
\rho
as input, and outputs a classical outcome
a
, together with the quantum state
\rho_{a}\coloneqq I_{a}[\rho]/\text{tr}(I_{a}[\rho])
, with probability
p_{a}\coloneqq\text{tr}(I_{a}[\rho])
. Quantum instruments can be equivalently represented by deterministic quantum channels, which instead of yielding a classical outcome, prepare an additional output pure quantum state
\ket{a}
that encodes the value of the instrument’s classical outcome
a
in a perfectly discriminable manner
[
59
,
60
,
61
]
. In other words, a quantum instrument with
N\in\mathbb{N}
outcomes
\{I_{a}\}_{a=1}^{N}
with
I_{a}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{I})\to\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{H}^{O})
is equivalent to a quantum channel
A:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{I})\to\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{H}^{O}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N})
given by

\displaystyle A[\,\rho\,]\coloneqq\sum_{a=1}^{N}I_{a}[\,\rho\,]\otimes\outerproduct{a}{a},

(66)

and illustrated in Fig.
B2
(b)
. By representing a quantum instrument as a particular case of a bipartite quantum channel, namely one with a single input system and two output systems, it is straightforward to see that the action of the quantum switch on a pair of instruments
\{I^{A}_{a}\}_{a}
and
\{I^{B}_{b}\}_{b}
constitutes a special case of its action on arbitrary bipartite channels
A
and
B
. Hence, a simulation of the quantum switch for all bipartite channels can be used for a simulation of the quantum switch for all instruments.

Lastly, one might also consider the scenario where the quantum switch acts on a part of bipartite instruments. Notice, however, that from the channel representation of instruments, it is clear that this scenario is included in that of arbitrary bipartite channels discussed in the previous section.

Figure B2:
General quantum channels and quantum instruments.

(a)
Quantum circuit representation of the Stinespring dilation
U_{A}
of the general quantum channel
A
presented in Eq. (
67
).
(b)
Quantum circuit representation of a quantum instrument
\{I_{a}\}_{a}
by a quantum channel
A[\rho]\coloneqq\sum_{a}I_{a}[\rho]\otimes\outerproduct{a}{a}
, which is then represented by its Stinespring dilation
U_{A}
.

B3
The relationship with Stinespring dilation of quantum channels

Figure B3:
The action of the quantum switch.
Depiction of the action of the quantum switch on
(a)
general single-party quantum channels;
(b)
general single-party quantum instruments;
(c)
part of general bipartite quantum channels, and;
(d)
part of general bipartite quantum instruments; as a function of the action of the quantum switch on the Stinespring dilation of the input channels.

The Stinespring dilation theorem
[
28
]
states that the action of every general quantum channel, i.e, CPTP map,
A:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{I})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{O})
can be written as

\displaystyle A[\,\rho\,]=\text{tr}_{\text{aux}_{O}}\Big(U_{A}[\,\rho^{I}\otimes\outerproduct{0}{0}\,]\Big),

(67)

for some unitary channel
U_{A}:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{I}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\text{aux}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{O}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\text{aux}_{O}})
that acts jointly on the input system and an auxiliary system (of sufficiently large dimension), which can be initialized to the state
\ket{0}
without loss of generality. We emphasize that the discarding of the output auxiliary system is necessary for the equivalence between non-unitary channels and their unitary channel dilation. This equivalence between arbitrary quantum channels and their unitary channel dilation is depicted in Fig.
B2
(a)
. Additionally, note that the Stinespring dilation can be combined with the channel representation of a quantum instrument presented in Eq. (
66
), so that all instruments can also be viewed as a unitary channel that makes use of an auxiliary system which is later discarded, as depicted in Fig.
B2
(b)
.

Exploiting the Stinespring dilation of general quantum instruments and general quantum channels into bipartite unitary channels that discard their auxiliary system, one can express the action—and consequently the simulation—of the quantum switch solely in terms of unitary channels. However, in order to do so, it is necessary to keep track of exactly which systems are acted upon, preserved, and discarded. In Fig.
B3
, we show precisely how the action of the quantum switch on the different kinds of inputs discussed so far can be expressed in terms of its action on unitary channels.

In Fig.
B3
(a)
, we depict the action of the quantum switch on general single-party channels. In Fig.
B3
(b)
, we represent the action of the quantum switch on single-party quantum instruments. In Fig.
B3
(c)
, we show the action of the switch in its most general case: when it acts on only part of bipartite general channels. Finally, in Fig.
B3
(d)
, the action of the switch on part of bipartite instruments is pictured. Trivially, the case in Fig.
B3
(a)
is a particular case of Fig.
B3
(c)
. Notice, moreover, how both the cases of Fig.
B3
(b)
and
(d)
, concerning quantum instruments, are also particular cases of Fig.
B3
(c)
. In the latter case in particular, this is because the dimension of the primed spaces of the input bipartite channels—the ones that the switch does not act upon—are arbitrary, and hence, the quantum outputs
\ket{a}
and
\ket{b}
of the instruments can be absorbed into the other output system of the unitary channels that is not acted upon by the quantum switch.

B4
Theorem
1
: A simulation for bipartite unitary channels
U_{A}
and bipartite general channels
B

In this section, we prove Thm.
1
from the main text and discuss how this particular-case simulation compares to more general simulation scenarios. We start by reproducing Eq. (
6
) of the main text which defines a general simulation of the quantum switch. Namely, a simulation
\mathcal{C}
that is able to prepare, with some finite number of calls
k_{A}
and
k_{B}
, a channel
\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes k_{A}},B^{\otimes k_{B}})
such that

\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes k_{A}},B^{\otimes k_{B}})=\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}(A,B)\ \ \ \forall\ A,B,

(68)

where
A,B
are arbitrary quantum channels, is a higher-order transformation that can simulate the action of the quantum switch in its most general form.

We are now ready to restate and prove Theorem
1
:

Theorem 1
.

The action of the quantum switch on part of bipartite quantum channels can be deterministically simulated by a quantum circuit that has access to an extra call to one the input channels, as long as that channel is restricted to being unitary.

In other words, if
A
is a bipartite unitary channel and
B
is a bipartite general channel, there exists a quantum circuit described by a higher-order transformation
\mathcal{C}
that satisfies Eq. (
68
) for
k_{A}=2
and
k_{B}=1
.

The proof is based on the explicit construction of the following quantum circuit, presented as Eq. (
7
) in the main text and repeated here for convenience:

c_{I}

c_{O}

{\text{aux}_{1}}_{I}

{\text{aux}_{1}}_{O}

{\text{aux}_{2}}_{I}

{\text{aux}_{2}}_{O}

A^{\prime}_{I}

A

A

A

A

A^{\prime}_{O}

t_{I}

B

B

t_{O}

B^{\prime}_{I}

B^{\prime}_{O}

(69)

Proof.

Let
A:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}})
be a bipartite channel with Kraus operators
\{\mathsf{A}_{i}\}
where
\mathsf{A}_{i}:\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}}
. Similarly, let
B:\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}})
be a bipartite channel with Kraus operators
\{\mathsf{B}_{j}\}
where
\mathsf{B}_{j}:\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}}
. The action of the quantum switch
\mathcal{S}
on part of these bipartite channels results in the quantum channel
\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}(A,B):\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{c_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}})\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}})
, whose definition is implied by the action of the quantum switch on single-party channels and linearity. It is given by

\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}(A,B)[\,\sigma_{c}\otimes\rho_{t}\otimes\omega_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\,]=\sum_{i,j}\mathsf{S}_{ij}(\sigma_{c}\otimes\rho_{t}\otimes\omega_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}})\mathsf{S}_{ij}^{\dagger},

(70)

where
\omega_{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}})
is an arbitrary state of the quantum system in the primed spaces of Alice and Bob, which are not acted upon by the quantum switch. Before explicitly writing the
\mathsf{S}_{ij}
, it is convenient to decompose the Kraus operators
\mathsf{A}_{i}
and
\mathsf{B}_{i}
into linear combinations of operators that factorize between the primed and nonprimed spaces, which can always be done without loss of generality. It follows from linearity that one can always write

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{i}=\sum_{a}\alpha_{a|i}\,\mathsf{A}(a)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)

(71)

for some
\alpha_{a|i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\mathsf{A}(a):\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}
, and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a):\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{A^{\prime}_{O}}
. Similarly, one can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{j}=\sum_{b}\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{B}(b)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)

(72)

for some
\beta_{b|j}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\mathsf{B}(b):\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}
, and
\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b):\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{I}}\to\mathcal{H}^{B^{\prime}_{O}}
.
Then, the Kraus operators
\{\mathsf{S}_{ij}\}
in Eq. (
70
) take the form

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{ij}=\outerproduct{0}{0}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|i}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{B}(b)\mathsf{A}(a)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)+\outerproduct{1}{1}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|i}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{A}(a)\mathsf{B}(b)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b),

(73)

where the order of the spaces from left to right in each term is control, target, and Alice and Bob’s primed systems.

The transformation of the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
) that uses two calls of the quantum channel
A
and a single call of the quantum channel
B
in the order ABA results in a quantum channel
\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes 2},B)
which has Kraus operators

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{ijk}=\outerproduct{0}{0}\otimes\mathsf{A}_{k}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|i}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}(b)\mathsf{A}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)+\outerproduct{1}{1}\otimes\mathsf{A}_{i}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|k}\,\beta_{b|j}\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{A}(a)\mathsf{B}(b)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b),

(74)

where the order of the spaces from left to right in each term coincides with the order of the wires from top to bottom in the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
) (i.e., control, first auxiliary, second auxiliary, Alice’s primed, target, and Bob’s primed systems).

Whenever the bipartite channel
A=U_{A}
is unitary, it has a single Kraus operator, i.e.,
A[\rho]=\mathsf{A}_{0}\rho\mathsf{A}_{0}^{\dagger}
. Hence, following Eq. (
73
), in this case the quantum channel resulting from action of the quantum switch
\mathcal{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}(U_{A},B)
has Kraus operators

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{j}=\outerproduct{0}{0}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|0}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{B}(b)\mathsf{A}(a)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)+\outerproduct{1}{1}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|0}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{A}(a)\mathsf{B}(b)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b).

(75)

Also in this case, following Eq. (
74
), the quantum channel resulting from action of the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
) is described by the Kraus operators

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{j}

\displaystyle=\outerproduct{0}{0}\otimes\mathsf{A}_{0}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|0}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}(b)\mathsf{A}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)+\outerproduct{1}{1}\otimes\mathsf{A}_{0}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|0}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{A}(a)\mathsf{B}(b)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)

(76)

\displaystyle=\left(\outerproduct{0}{0}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|0}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{B}(b)\mathsf{A}(a)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)+\outerproduct{1}{1}\otimes\sum_{ab}\alpha_{a|0}\,\beta_{b|j}\,\mathsf{A}(a)\mathsf{B}(b)\otimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(a)\otimes\mathsf{B}^{\prime}(b)\right)\otimes\mathsf{A}_{0}.

(77)

where, in the last line, we reordered the spaces from control, first auxiliary, second auxiliary, Alice’s primed, target, and Bob’s primed systems to control, target, and Alice and Bob’s primed, first auxiliary, and second auxiliary systems.

Note that the term in parenthesis is equal to that of Eq. (
75
). Hence, the action of the quantum switch on part of a bipartite unitary channel
A=U_{A}
and a bipartite general channel
B
is equivalent to the action of the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
) that uses two calls of bipartite unitary channel
A=U_{A}
and a single call of bipartite general channel
B
in the order ABA. Moreover, note that one call of
A=U_{A}
in the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
) is recovered, since
A_{0}
factorizes in Eq. (
76
). This phenomenon is referred to as a catalytic higher-order transformation in the literature
[
62
]
, since the extra use of
A=U_{A}
is recovered after the completion of the process.
∎

We now contrast the different scenarios of the action of the quantum switch presented in Secs.
B1
–
B3
with the particular-case simulation of Theorem
1
.

Let us begin with the circuit from Ref.
[
6
]
, reproduced in Eq. (
4
) of the main text, and depicted here as the quantum comb in Fig.
B4
(a)
. This circuit simulates the action of the quantum switch on unitary channels by making use of one extra call to one of the input channels. Although every general quantum channel can be dilated into a unitary channel acting on a larger space, the simulation of the quantum switch on unitary channels in the case depicted in Fig.
B4
(a)
is not the most general case, because here the
entire
unitary channel is “plugged into” the open slots of the quantum switch and of the quantum comb that performs its simulation.

The simulation presented in Ref.
[
6
]
, here in Fig.
B4
(a)
, is a particular case of the simulation scenario we proved possible via the explicit construction of the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
). Our generalization addresses the case where the quantum switch acts on only part of a unitary channel and part of a general quantum channel, with the simulation also requiring only one extra copy of the input unitary channel. We depict this scenario and our quantum circuit simulation as a quantum comb in Fig.
B4
(b)
. Although more general than the previous result from Ref.
[
6
]
, this simulation result still does not hold in the most general case, depicted in Fig.
B1
(c)
, because the input quantum channel
U_{A}
is required to be a unitary channel.

Figure B4:
Quantum comb representation of possible quantum switch simulations.

(a)
Representation of the simulation of the action of the quantum switch on single-party unitary channels as a quantum comb, presented in Ref.
[
6
]
and reproduced in the main text as the quantum circuit in Eq. (
4
)
(b)
Representation of the simulation of the action of the quantum switch on part of a bipartite unitary channel and part of a bipartite general quantum channel by a quantum comb, which we have shown to be possible by explicitly constructing the quantum circuit in Eq. (
69
) in Thm.
1
.

The quantum switch simulation presented in Fig.
B4
(b)
is also useful to illustrate the crucial aspect of the partial trace involved in the Stinespring dilation. Notice that this simulation covers the scenario where
B
is an arbitrary bipartite channel, and
U_{A}
is an arbitrary bipartite unitary channel. Naïvely, one could expect that—since our simulation covers all bipartite unitary channels
U_{A}
—due to the Stinespring dilation theorem, this simulation would also apply to the case where
A
is a single-party arbitrary quantum channel, as in Fig.
B3
(a)
. However, this line of argumentation is false, as it is not possible to simulate the quantum switch for a pair of single-party arbitrary quantum channels, as proven in the main text. The logical gap in the argument is a misuse of the Stinespring dilation theorem, which requires the auxiliary system to be discarded, whereas in the simulation presented in Fig.
B4
(b)
, there is no partial trace after the unitary operation
U_{A}
. When the auxiliary system of the Stinespring dilation is not discarded, the corresponding operation is not equivalent to the quantum channel in question. From this circuit simulation perspective, keeping track of the auxiliary systems may be viewed as a “loophole”, since the auxiliary system may carry additional information that is not provided by the channel
A
. To see this intuitively, consider a quantum channel
A
with a Kraus decomposition given by
A[\rho]=\sum_{i}\mathsf{K}_{i}\rho\mathsf{K}_{i}^{\dagger}
and which is dilated by the isometry
\mathsf{V}=\sum_{i}\mathsf{K}_{i}\otimes\ket{i}
, where
\ket{i}
is a quantum state on the auxiliary system. The quantum state
\ket{i}
may be viewed as a flag that indicates which Kraus operator was applied to
\rho
. If the flag state is not discarded, one could use this information to correlate Kraus elements between a first and a second call of the channel
A
, which cannot be done when the auxiliary system is traced out.

Appendix C
Full proof of Theorem
2

This section alone will adopt a slightly different notation from the main text and the remainder of the Appendix. This is due to the fact that the proof technique of this particular theorem is different from those applied to prove and present the other results, and it benefits from a different notation. Below is a summary of the changes, to facilitate the transition into this next section:

•

We will denote a Hilbert space simply by the label of the system it describes, according to
X\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{X}
. In particular, the input and output Hilbert spaces of the control and target systems in the quantum switch higher-order transformation will be denoted instead as “past” and “future”, using the notation
P_{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{c_{I}}
,
P_{T}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{t_{I}}
,
F_{C}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}}
,
F_{T}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{t_{O}}
, and collectively as
P\coloneqq P_{C}\otimes P_{T}
and
F\coloneqq F_{C}\otimes F_{T}
.

•

Quantum channels will be denoted by cursive letters
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
instead of
A
and
B
. Kraus operators and unitary operators will be denoted by
A_{i}
,
B_{j}
and
U
,
V
instead of
\mathsf{A}_{i}
,
\mathsf{B}_{j}
and
\mathsf{U}
,
\mathsf{V}
. Higher-order transformations will continue to be denoted with cursive letters, such as
\mathcal{S}
for the quantum switch and
\mathcal{C}
for quantum combs or QC-CC simulators, as in the remainder of the Appendix.

•

Higher-order transformations that correspond to the simulator, be it a quantum comb or a QC-CC, will be denoted as a function
\mathcal{C}
that has as many arguments as there are slots in the transformation (e.g.,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})
for a
3
-slot higher-order transformation). The number of available calls to channel
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
will be denoted as
M
(instead of
k_{A}
) and
N
(instead of
k_{B}
). Hence, what would be written as
\mathcal{C}(A^{\otimes{k_{A}}},B^{\otimes{k_{B}}})
in the remainder of the Appendix, in this section becomes instead
\mathcal{C}(\underbrace{\mathcal{A},\dots,\mathcal{A}}_{M},\underbrace{\mathcal{B},\dots,\mathcal{B}}_{N})
.

•

Choi operators, Choi vectors, and the link product will be used as defined in Sec. IV Methods of the main text.

We are now ready to restate and prove Thm.
2
.

Theorem 2

(expanded)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}:[\mathcal{L}(I)\to\mathcal{L}(O)]\otimes[\mathcal{L}(I^{\prime})\to\mathcal{L}(O^{\prime})]\to[\mathcal{L}(P)\to\mathcal{L}(F)]
, where
I,O,I^{\prime},O^{\prime}
are
n
-qubit Hilbert spaces and
P,F
are
(n+1)
-qubit Hilbert spaces, be the quantum switch.

There is no (
M+1
)-slot quantum circuit with classical control of causal order (QC-CC) higher-order transformation
\mathcal{C}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}[\mathcal{L}(I_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(O_{i})]\otimes[\mathcal{L}(I^{\prime}_{1})\to\mathcal{L}(O^{\prime}_{1})]\to[\mathcal{L}(P)\to\mathcal{L}(F)]
, where
\{I_{i}\}_{i},\{O_{i}\}_{i},I^{\prime}_{1},O^{\prime}_{1}
are
n
-qubit Hilbert spaces, satisfying

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\underbrace{\mathcal{A},\dots,\mathcal{A}}_{M},\mathcal{B})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

(78)

for all mixed unitary channels
\mathcal{A}
and unitary channels
\mathcal{B}
, if
M\leq\max(2,2^{n}-1)
.

Proof.

The proof is based upon a series of lemmas, proven below. First, we assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a multilinear higher-order transformation whose Choi operator
C
is positive semidefinite, and hence can be written as

\displaystyle C=\sum_{a}|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|

(79)

with
|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|\geq 0
for all
a
.

Let us write the mixed unitary channel
\mathcal{A}
in Eq. (
78
) as
\mathcal{A}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, for some
M\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, where
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i}
are unitary channels with associated unitary operators
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
, and let unitary channel
\mathcal{B}
be associated to unitary operator
V_{1}
. We then invoke Lemma
2
(which requires Lemma
1
for its proof) for
N=1
to say that, since it is assumed that
\mathcal{C}
satisfies Eq. (
78
), each
|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|
satisfies

\displaystyle|C^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle\ast\Big(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\xi^{(a)}_{k1}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ V_{1})\,|S\rangle\!\rangle\ast\Big(|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle\Big)\,

(80)

for some coefficients
\xi^{(a)}_{k1}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ V_{1})\in\mathbb{C}
, where
|S\rangle\!\rangle
is the Choi vector of the quantum switch.

From Lemma
3
, we know that if there exist coefficients
\xi^{(a)}_{k1}\leftarrow\tilde{\xi}^{(a)}_{k1}
such that, for all
k\in\{1,\dots,M\}
,
\tilde{\xi}^{(a)}_{k1}
is simultaneously

1.

independent of
U_{k}
and
V_{1}
, and

2.

linear in
U_{k^{\prime}}
for all
k^{\prime}\neq k
,

then, there exist vectors
|\tilde{\xi}^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1},\cdots,I_{M}O_{M}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}\}}
independent of
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
and
V_{1}
such that

\displaystyle|C^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{1}O_{1}\cdots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}F}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}|S\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}F}\otimes|\tilde{\xi}^{(a)}_{k1}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1},\cdots,I_{M}O_{M}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}\}}

(81)

for all
a
. Such conditions are guaranteed to be satisfied via Lemma
4
when
M<4^{n}/2+2
[which is implied by
M\leq\max(2,2^{n}-1)
].

Finally, invoking Lemma
5
(which requires Lemmas
6
and
7
for its proof), we find that if
M\leq\max(2,2^{n}-1)
, a higher-order transformation with Choi operator
C=\sum_{a}|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|
, where
|C^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle
satisfies Eqs. (
81
) for all
a
does not satisfy the conditions of a QC-CC transformation.
∎

C1
Lemma
1

Lemma 1
.

Let
\ket{\phi}
and
\{\ket{\psi_i}\}_{i}
be vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{d}
.

\displaystyle\text{If }\outerproduct{\phi}{\phi}\leq\sum_{i}\outerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_i},\;\text{then}\ket{\phi}\in\mathrm{span}(\{\ket{\psi_i}\}).

(82)

That is, there exist complex numbers
\alpha_{i}
such that
\ket{\phi}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\ket{\psi_i}
.

Proof.

The proof will go by contradiction. We start by pointing out that any vector
\ket{\phi}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\ket{\phi}=\ket{\psi}+\ket{\psi_\perp},

(83)

where
\ket{\psi}\in\mathrm{span}(\{\ket{\psi_i}\})
,
\ket{\psi_\perp}\notin\mathrm{span}(\{\ket{\psi_i}\})
. Also, since
\ket{\psi_\perp}\notin\mathrm{span}(\{\ket{\psi_i}\})
, we have that
\innerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi_i}=0
for every
i
.

Now, assume that
\ket{\phi}\notin\mathrm{span}(\{\ket{\psi_i}\})
. In this case, we necessarily have that
\ket{\psi_\perp}\neq 0
.
Using this decomposition
\ket{\phi}=\ket{\psi}+\ket{\psi_\perp}
, we can write the inequality
\outerproduct{\phi}{\phi}\leq\sum_{i}\outerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_i},
as

\displaystyle\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}+\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi_\perp}+\outerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi}+\outerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi_\perp}\leq\sum_{i}\outerproduct{\psi_i}{\psi_i}.

(84)

We then apply
\bra{\psi_\perp}
and
\ket{\psi_\perp}
on both sides of operator inequality in Eq. (
84
) to obtain the real number inequality

\displaystyle\innerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi_\perp}\innerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi_\perp}\leq 0.

(85)

However, since
\ket{\psi_\perp}\neq 0
,
\innerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi_\perp}\innerproduct{\psi_\perp}{\psi_\perp}
is strictly positive, hence we have arrived at a contradiction. Therefore,
\ket{\phi}
must belong to the
\mathrm{span}(\{\ket{\psi_i}\})
.
∎

C2
Lemma
2
(for
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
)

Lemma 2
.

Let
\mathcal{S}:[\mathcal{L}(I)\to\mathcal{L}(O)]\otimes[\mathcal{L}(I^{\prime})\to\mathcal{L}(O^{\prime})]\to[\mathcal{L}(P)\to\mathcal{L}(F)]
, where
I,O,I^{\prime},O^{\prime}
correspond to
d
-dimensional Hilbert spaces and
P,F
correspond to
(2\times d)
-dimensional Hilbert spaces, be a multilinear higher-order transformation that acts on two quantum channels and has a rank-
1
Choi operator
S=|{S}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{S}|
(such as, e.g., the quantum switch). Furthermore, let
\mathcal{C}:\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}[\mathcal{L}(I_{i})\to\mathcal{L}(O_{i})]\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}[\mathcal{L}(I^{\prime}_{j})\to\mathcal{L}(O^{\prime}_{j})]\to[\mathcal{L}(P)\to\mathcal{L}(F)]
, where
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\{I_{i}\}_{i},\{O_{i}\}_{i},\{I^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j},\{O^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j}
correspond to
d
-dimensional Hilbert spaces, be a multilinear higher-order transformation that acts on
M+N
quantum channels with a positive semidefinite Choi operator
C
, which, therefore, can be written as

\displaystyle C=\sum_{a}|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|\,,

(86)

where each
|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|\geq 0
.
Finally, let
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
be mixed unitary channels, such that
\mathcal{A}=\frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{B}=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{j=1}^{L}\mathcal{B}_{j}
for some
K,L\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, where
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j}
are unitary channels with associated unitary operators
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{V_{j}\}_{j}
, respectively.

If
\mathcal{C}
corresponds to a simulation of the action of
\mathcal{S}
on mixed unitary channels
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, i.e., if
\mathcal{C}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(\underbrace{\mathcal{A},\dots,\mathcal{A}}_{M},\underbrace{\mathcal{B},\dots,\mathcal{B}}_{N})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

(87)

for all mixed unitary channels
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
(or, if
N=1
, for all mixed unitary channels
\mathcal{A}
and unitary channels
\mathcal{B}
), then, for every
a
, there exist coefficients
\{\xi^{(a)}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})\}_{kl}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle|C_{a}\rangle\!\rangle\ast\left(|U_{1}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes\ldots\otimes|U_{M}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes\ldots\otimes|V_{N}\rangle\!\rangle\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\xi^{(a)}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})|S\rangle\!\rangle\ast\left(|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{l}\rangle\!\rangle\right)\,.

(88)

Proof.

Since, by assumption, Eq. (
87
) holds for all mixed unitary channels
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, for any sets of unitary channels
\{\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots\mathcal{A}_{K}\}
and
\{\mathcal{B}_{1},\dots\mathcal{B}_{L}\}
with
K,L\geq 1
, one has that

\mathcal{C}\left(\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{K}\frac{\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}}{K},\dots,\sum_{i_{M}=1}^{K}\frac{\mathcal{A}_{i_{M}}}{K};\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{L}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{j_{1}}}{L},\dots,\sum_{j_{N}=1}^{L}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{j_{N}}}{L}\right)=\mathcal{S}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\mathcal{A}_{k}}{K},\sum_{l=1}^{L}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{l}}{L}\right)\,.

(89)

Note that for
N=1
, it is sufficient to assume that Eq. (
87
) holds for all mixed unitary channels
\mathcal{A}
and unitary channels
\mathcal{B}
, in which case we take
L=1
.

From the multilinearity of
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{K^{M}L^{N}}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{M}=1}^{K}\;\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{N}=1}^{L}\mathcal{C}\left(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{i_{M}};\mathcal{B}_{j_{1}},\dots,\mathcal{B}_{j_{N}}\right)=\frac{1}{KL}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\mathcal{S}\left(\mathcal{A}_{k},\mathcal{B}_{l}\right)\,.

(90)

Rewriting this expression in the Choi representation—using the convention that
C,S,A_{i},B_{j}
are the Choi matrices of
\mathcal{C},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{B}_{j}
, respectively—gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{M}\text{$=1$}}^{K}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{N}=1}^{L}C\ast\left(A_{i_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes A_{i_{M}}\otimes B_{j_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes B_{j_{N}}\right)=(K^{M-1}L^{N-1})\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{L}S\ast\left(A_{k}\otimes B_{l}\right)\,.

(91)

Now, using the decomposition of
C
given by Eq. (
86
), and
S=|{S}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{S}|
, one has that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{a}|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|\ast\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{M}=1}^{K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},\dots,j_{N}=1\end{subarray}}^{L}\left(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}A_{i_{k}}\otimes\bigotimes_{l=1}^{N}B_{j_{l}}\right)=\,(K^{M-1}L^{N-1})|{S}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{S}|\ast\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left(A_{k}\otimes B_{l}\right)\,.\end{split}

(92)

The positivity of each
|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|
implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|\ast\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{M}=1}^{K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},\dots,j_{N}=1\end{subarray}}^{L}\left(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}A_{i_{k}}\otimes\bigotimes_{l=1}^{N}B_{j_{l}}\right)\leq(K^{M-1}L^{N-1})|{S}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{S}|\ast\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l=1}^{L}\left(A_{k}\otimes B_{l}\right)\end{split}

(93)

for all
a
.

Consider now the case where
K=M,L=N
.
Since
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
are Choi operators of unitary channels, they can be expressed as
A_{i}=|{U_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U_{i}}|
and
B_{j}=|{V_{j}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{j}}|
. Since the left-hand side of Eq. (
93
) is a sum of positive operators, the inequality also holds for the sum of any subset of the terms on the left-hand side. Then, by considering only the term in the sum over
i_{1},\dots,i_{M},j_{1},\dots,j_{N}
that corresponds to
i_{k}=k,j_{l}=l
for all
k,l
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\begin{split}|{C^{(a)}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}}|\ast\Big(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}|{U_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U_{k}}|\otimes\bigotimes_{l=1}^{N}|{V_{l}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{l}}|\Big)\leq(M^{(M-1)}N^{(N-1)})|{S}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{S}|\ast\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\left(|{U_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{U_{k}}|\otimes|{V_{l}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{V_{l}}|\right)\,,\end{split}

(94)

for all
a
. Following the link product rule for vectors, as in, e.g., Lemma 1 of Ref.
[
52
]
, the above equation can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}\bigotimes_{l=1}^{N}(|C^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle\ast|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{l}\rangle\!\rangle)(\langle\!\langle C^{(a)}|\ast\langle\!\langle U_{k}|\otimes\langle\!\langle V_{l}|)\leq(M^{(M-1)}N^{(N-1)})\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}(|S\rangle\!\rangle\ast|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{l}\rangle\!\rangle)(\langle\!\langle S|\ast\langle\!\langle U_{k}|\otimes\langle\!\langle V_{l}|)\,.\end{split}

(95)

Now, invoking Lemma
1
, we arrive at the conclusion that

\displaystyle|C^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle\ast\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}|V_{j}\rangle\!\rangle\in\text{span}(\{|S\rangle\!\rangle\ast\left(|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{l}\rangle\!\rangle\right)\}_{kl})\,,

(96)

for all
a
. Therefore, for every
a
, we have that

\displaystyle|C^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle\ast\Big(\bigotimes_{k=1}^{M}|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}|V_{j}\rangle\!\rangle\Big)=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\xi^{(a)}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})\,|S\rangle\!\rangle\ast\left(|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes|V_{l}\rangle\!\rangle\right)\,,

(97)

for some coefficients
\xi^{(a)}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})\in\mathbb{C}
for all
k,l
.

∎

C3
Lemma
3
(for
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
)

Lemma 3
.

Let
|S\rangle\!\rangle\in P\otimes I\otimes O\otimes I^{\prime}\otimes O^{\prime}\otimes F
, where
I,O,I^{\prime},O^{\prime}
correspond to
d
-dimensional Hilbert spaces and
P,F
correspond to
(2\times d)
-dimensional Hilbert spaces, and let
|C\rangle\!\rangle\in P\otimes I_{1}\otimes O_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{M}\otimes O_{M}\otimes I^{\prime}_{1}\otimes O^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes I^{\prime}_{N}\otimes O^{\prime}_{N}\otimes F
, where
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\{I_{i}\}_{i},\{O_{i}\}_{i},\{I^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j},\{O^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j}
correspond to
d
-dimensional Hilbert spaces.

If, for a given
M,N
, for all
(M+N)
-tuples of
n
-qubit unitary operators
(U_{1},\dots,U_{M},V_{1},\dots,V_{N})
,

|C\rangle\!\rangle*|U_{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}O_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes|U_{M}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{M}O_{M}}\otimes|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes|V_{N}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\tilde{\xi}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})|S\rangle\!\rangle*|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}\otimes|V_{l}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}}

(98)

for some coefficients
\tilde{\xi}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})\in\mathbb{C}
for all
k,l
that are simultaneously

1.

independent of
U_{k}
and
V_{l}
, and

2.

linear in
U_{i}
and
V_{j}
for all
i\neq k,j\neq l
,

then

\displaystyle|C\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}F}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}|S\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}F}\otimes|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}\}}

(99)

for some vectors
|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}\}}
that are independent of
\{U_{i}\}_{i}
and
\{V_{j}\}_{j}
.

Proof.

The first condition (independence) implies that we can write
\tilde{\xi}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j})=\tilde{\xi}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i\neq k},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j\neq l})
.
The second condition (linearity) combined with the Riesz representation theorem implies that we can write the linear functions
\tilde{\xi}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i\neq k},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j\neq l})
using vectors
|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle
according to

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{kl}(\{U_{i}\}_{i\neq k},\ \{V_{j}\}_{j\neq l})

\displaystyle=|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle\ast\bigotimes_{i\neq k}|U_{i}\rangle\!\rangle\otimes\bigotimes_{j\neq l}|V_{j}\rangle\!\rangle\,,

(100)

where the definition of the link product of two vectors
\ket{\psi},\ket{\phi}\in\mathbb{C}^{d}
acting on the same space implies that
\ket{\psi}*\ket{\phi}=\overline{\bra{\psi}}\ket{\phi}
, where the bar denotes complex conjugation.

Then, by explicitly writing in the system labels, for any sets of
n
-qubit unitaries
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}
and
\{V_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
, Eq. (
98
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&|C\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}F}\ast\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}|U_{i}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}|V_{j}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{j}O^{\prime}_{j}}\right]\\
&=\left[\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}\}}\otimes|S\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}F}\right]\ast\left[\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}|U_{i}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}\otimes\bigotimes_{j=1}^{N}|V_{j}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{j}O^{\prime}_{j}}\right]\,,\end{split}

(101)

for some vectors
|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}\}}
.

Since the equation is true for all unitaries
\{U_{i}\}
and
\{V_{j}\}
, and the
\mathrm{span}(\{|U\rangle\!\rangle\,|\,U\in\text{SU}(d)\})=\mathbb{C}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{d}
, it implies that

\displaystyle|C\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}F}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{l=1}^{N}|S\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}F}\otimes|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}\}}

(102)

for some vectors
|\tilde{\xi}_{kl}\rangle\!\rangle^{\{I_{1}O_{1}\ldots I_{M}O_{M}I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}\ldots I^{\prime}_{N}O^{\prime}_{N}\}\setminus\{I_{k}O_{k}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}\}}
.
∎

C4
Lemma
4
(for
M<4^{n}/2+2
and
N=1
)

Lemma 4
.

Let
|S\rangle\!\rangle\in P\otimes I\otimes O\otimes I^{\prime}\otimes O^{\prime}\otimes F
, where
I,O,I^{\prime},O^{\prime}
correspond to
n
-qubit Hilbert spaces for some
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
P,F
correspond to
(n+1)
-qubit Hilbert spaces, and let
|C\rangle\!\rangle\in P\otimes I_{1}\otimes O_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{M}\otimes O_{M}\otimes I^{\prime}_{1}\otimes O^{\prime}_{1}\otimes F
, where
M\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\{I_{i}\}_{i},\{O_{i}\}_{i},I^{\prime}_{1},O^{\prime}_{1}
correspond to
n
-qubit Hilbert spaces.

If, for all
(M+1)
-tuples of
n
-qubit unitary operators
(U_{1},\dots,U_{M},V_{1})
, it holds that

|C\rangle\!\rangle*|U_{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}O_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes|U_{M}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{M}O_{M}}\otimes|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\xi_{k1}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|U_{k}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}\otimes|V_{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}

(103)

for some complex numbers
\xi_{k1}\coloneqq\xi_{k1}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ V_{1})\in\mathbb{C}
, then there exist complex numbers
\tilde{\xi}_{k1}\coloneqq\tilde{\xi}_{k1}(\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ V_{1})\in\mathbb{C}
such that Eq. (
103
) with
\{{\xi}_{k1}\}_{k=1}^{M}\leftarrow\{\tilde{\xi}_{k1}\}_{k=1}^{M}
remains satisfied and, for all
k\in\{1,\dots,M\}
,
\tilde{\xi}_{k1}
is simultaneously

1.

independent of
U_{k}
and
V_{1}
(independence condition), and

2.

linear in
U_{i}
for all
i\neq k
(linearity condition),

as long as
M<4^{n}/2+2
.

Proof.

Assume that Eq. (
103
) holds. Then, in particular, it holds for the choice
U_{i}=\sigma_{\vec{r_{i}}}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
and
V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}
, where
\vec{r}_{i},\vec{q}_{1}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}
and
\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i},\ \sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}
are
n
-qubit Pauli operators.
Here, the set of
n
-qubit Pauli operators is defined by

\displaystyle\left\{\sigma_{\vec{r}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{r_{i}}\middle|\vec{r}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}\right\},

(104)

where
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}
are 1-qubit Pauli operators defined by

\displaystyle\sigma_{0}\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right),\quad\sigma_{1}\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{matrix}\right),\quad\sigma_{2}\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{matrix}\right),\quad\sigma_{3}\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{matrix}\right).

(105)

Thus,

|C\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}O_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{M}O_{M}}\otimes|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\xi_{k1}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}\otimes|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}.

(106)

Now suppose that
F\geq 1
elements of the
\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{M}
are equal to some fixed
n
-qubit Pauli operator
\sigma_{\vec{w}}
. Let the set of integers labeling those Pauli operators be denoted by
\mathbb{F}\coloneqq\{1\leq i\leq M|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}=\sigma_{\vec{w}}\}
, such that
|\mathbb{F}|=F
. Equation (
103
) then reads

\begin{split}|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{i\in\mathbb{F}}|\sigma_{\vec{w}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}\otimes\bigotimes_{i\in\{1,\dots,M\}\backslash\mathbb{F}}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}\otimes|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}\otimes|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\,,\end{split}

(107)

where, for the input unitaries chosen as
n
-qubit Paulis, we define

\displaystyle\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}\coloneqq\xi_{k1}(\{U_{i}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{M},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}})\ .

(108)

In the following, we will adopt the shorthand convention that any changes to the dependence of
\xi_{k1}
from
\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}
will be specified as
\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[U_{i}=(\dots),V_{j}=(\dots)]
, with all unspecified arguments
U_{i},V_{1}
defined to be the same as for
\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}
defined above.
A key point that we note for later is that the value of each individual variable
\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}
for
k\in\mathbb{F}
is not uniquely determined from Eq. (
103
), so we can take a different set of variables
\tilde{\xi}_{k1}
still satisfying Eq. (
103
).

We now show that for all
k\in\{1,\dots,M\}
, the variables
\xi_{k1}
can be
replaced by
\tilde{\xi}_{k1}
defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{k1}(\{U_{i}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{M},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[U_{k}=\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}^{*}}]\,,

(109)

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{k1}\left(\left\{U_{i}=\sum_{\vec{r}_{i}}{\alpha_{\vec{r}_{i}}^{i}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}}\right\}_{i=1}^{M},\ V_{1}=\sum_{\vec{q}_{1}}\beta_{\vec{q}_{1}}^{1}\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l\neq k}}\left(\prod_{j\neq k}\alpha^{j}_{\vec{r}_{j}}\right)\tilde{\xi}_{k1}(\{U_{i}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{M},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}})\,,

(110)

where
\vec{r}^{*}_{k}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}
is an arbitrary vector
outside
of the set
\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{k-1},\vec{r}_{k+1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}
,
\vec{q}^{*}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}
is an arbitrary fixed vector, and
\alpha_{\vec{r}_{i}}^{i},\beta_{\vec{q}_{1}}^{1}
are complex numbers.
In the discussion below, we pick one choice of
\vec{r}_{k}^{*}
defined as a function of
\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{k-1},\vec{r}_{k+1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}
, i.e.,
\vec{r}_{k}^{*}=\vec{r}_{k}^{*}(\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{k-1},\vec{r}_{k+1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M})
. Such an
\vec{r}_{k}^{*}
always exists for
M<4^{n}
.

By construction, if the definition in Eqs. (
109
)–(
110
) satisfies Eq. (
103
), then
\tilde{\xi}_{k1}
satisfies both the linearity and independence conditions outlined in the statement of the lemma. We now proceed to show that the definition in Eqs. (
109
)–(
110
) indeed satisfies Eq. (
103
). We do this by considering the dependence of
\xi_{k1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}
on the unitaries
\{U_{i}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{M}
and
V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}
in turn.

Dependence on
\{U_{i}\}_{i=1}^{M}

We focus on the case where
\{U_{i}\}_{i},\ V_{1}
are chosen from the set of Pauli operators.
For every
k\in\{1,\dots M\}
, we choose one
\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}
and take
\sigma
to be the unique
n
-qubit Pauli operator such that
\sigma\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}=\beta\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}
, where
\beta\in\{-1,1,i,-i\}
. We then consider the following expression

\mathbf{E}\coloneqq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=0}\left[|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}|e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*\bigotimes_{m=1|m\notin\mathbb{F}_{k}}^{M}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\right]\,,

(111)

where
\mathbb{F}_{k}\coloneqq\{1\leq i\leq M\mid\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}
.
Due to linearity, this expression can be evaluated in two ways: either by first computing the derivative and then applying Eq. (
103
), or by first applying Eq. (
103
) and then computing the derivative. The former method gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=i\beta\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\left[|C\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*\bigotimes_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}|i\neq m}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}*\bigotimes_{i=1|i\notin\mathbb{F}_{k}}^{M}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=i\beta\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\Bigg[\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}|i\neq m}\xi_{i1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{i=1|i\notin\mathbb{F}_{k}}^{M}\xi_{i1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\Bigg]\,,

(112)

while the latter gives

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}

\displaystyle=\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=0}\Bigg[\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{m=1|m\notin\mathbb{F}_{k}}^{M}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=i\beta\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{l}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=0}\Bigg[\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]\Bigg]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{\vec{v}\in\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}\backslash\{\vec{r}_{k}\}}\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}\theta}\bigg|_{\theta=0}\Bigg[\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]\Bigg]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{v}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\,,

(113)

where
\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}\coloneqq\{1\leq m\leq M\mid\vec{r}_{m}=\vec{v}\}
. Note that the vector

\displaystyle|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}^{\prime}O^{\prime}_{1}}

(114)

belongs to the Hilbert space
P\otimes F
, which is independent of
I_{m},O_{m},I_{1}^{\prime},O^{\prime}_{1}
, thus the superscripts
I_{m},O_{m},I_{1}^{\prime},O^{\prime}_{1}
can be omitted. Also, note that

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]

(115)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]

(116)

are differentiable since their values are uniquely determined from

\displaystyle|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}|e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*\bigotimes_{m=1|m\notin\mathbb{F}_{k}}^{M}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{m}O_{m}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]\right)|S\rangle\!\rangle*|e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{\vec{v}\in\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}\backslash\{\vec{r}_{k}\}}\left(\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[\{U_{i}=e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\}_{i\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}]\right)|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{v}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\,,

(117)

and thus can be obtained from the inner product of the vector on the left-hand side of Eq. (
117
) and vectors
|S\rangle\!\rangle*|e^{i\theta\sigma}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle
or
|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{v}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle
(which are mutually orthogonal), which are polynomials of
e^{\pm i\theta}
.

By comparing the coefficients for
|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}^{*}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle
, we find that

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{k}}\xi_{m1}^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}[U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}^{*}}].

(118)

Dependence on
V_{1}

In this part of the proof, we adopt the following shorthand notations. For any unitary operators
U,V
,

\displaystyle|C[V]\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneqq|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}*|V\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}^{\prime}O_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle|S(U,V)\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneqq|S\rangle\!\rangle*|U\rangle\!\rangle*|V\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},V)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}[V_{1}=V]\,,

(119)

where
\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}\coloneqq\{1\leq i\leq M\mid\vec{r}_{i}=\vec{v}\}
.

For any two
n
-qubit Pauli operators
\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B}
, either the operator
(\sigma_{A}+\sigma_{B})/\sqrt{2}
or the operator
(\sigma_{A}+i\sigma_{B})/\sqrt{2}
is unitary.
When
U\coloneqq(\sigma_{A}+\beta\sigma_{B})/\sqrt{2}
with
\beta\in\{1,i\}
is unitary, the following equality holds for any
\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B}
:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle|C[U]\rangle\!\rangle-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|C[\sigma_{A}]\rangle\!\rangle+\beta|C[\sigma_{B}]\rangle\!\rangle)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{\vec{v}\in\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}}\left[\{b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)-b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})\}|S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})\rangle\!\rangle+\beta\{b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)-b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})\}|S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})\rangle\!\rangle\right].

(120)

We now calculate the inner product

\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}|{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}},\sigma_{B})}\rangle\!\rangle=\text{tr}(\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}})+\text{tr}(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}})\,.

(121)

From this, it is clear that
\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}|{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}},\sigma_{B})}\rangle\!\rangle=0
if
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
. Therefore, taking an inner product of Eq. (
120
) with
|S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})\rangle\!\rangle
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\{b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)-b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})\}\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}|{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}\rangle\!\rangle=0\textrm{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ if for all}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}:\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}\,\\
\\
\{b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)-b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})\}\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}|{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}\rangle\!\rangle\\
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\beta\{b(\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B},U)-b(\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B},\sigma_{B})\}\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}|{S(\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B},\sigma_{B})}\rangle\!\rangle=0\textrm{\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ else},\end{cases}

(122)

where
\gamma\in\{1,-1,i,-i\}
is defined by the unique choice of
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
such that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}=\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}\,.

(123)

In the first case, i.e., if for all
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}:\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
, we directly obtain

\displaystyle b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)-b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})=0\,.

(124)

In the second case, if

\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})}|{S(\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B},\sigma_{B})}\rangle\!\rangle=\text{tr}(\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})+\text{tr}(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})=0

(125)

holds, we also obtain Eq. (
124
)

By a similar argument, if for all
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}:\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}
, we directly obtain

\displaystyle b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)-b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})=0\,.

(126)

Alternatively, if

\langle\!\langle{S(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})}|{S(\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A},\sigma_{A})}\rangle\!\rangle=\text{tr}(\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{\vec{v}}\delta\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A})+\text{tr}(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\delta\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A})=0

(127)

holds, where
\delta\in\{1,-1,i,-i\}
is defined by the unique choice of
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
such that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}=\delta\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\,,

(128)

we also obtain Eq. (
126
).

Consider now the conditions for Eq. (
125
) to be satisfied. The first term is given by
\text{tr}(\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})=\gamma\text{tr}\mathbb{1}
. For the second term, there are four cases:

•

If
\gamma=\pm 1
, then
\pm\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
is an
n
-qubit Pauli operator so the second term is given by

\text{tr}[\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}\gamma\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}]=\gamma\text{tr}[(\pm\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})(\pm\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})]=\gamma\text{tr}\mathbb{1}
.

•

If
\gamma=\pm i
, then
\pm i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
is an
n
-qubit Pauli operator so the second term is given by

\text{tr}[(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})\gamma(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})]=-\gamma\text{tr}[(\pm i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})(\pm i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B})]=-\gamma\text{tr}\mathbb{1}
.

Therefore, Eq. (
125
) is satisfied if and only if
\gamma=\pm i
. By a similar argument, Eq. (
127
) is satisfied if and only if
\delta=\pm i
.

Note that the following equivalences hold:
\forall\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}:\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\iff\forall\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}:\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
, and also
\gamma\in\{i,-i\}\iff\delta\in\{i,-i\}
. Therefore, for all
\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
, for every tuple
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B})
, if one of the two following conditions is satisfied:

1.

\forall\,\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}:\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\not\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
, or

2.

\exists\,\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}
such that
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}=\pm i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}
,

then

\displaystyle b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)\,,

(129)

\displaystyle b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U)\,,

(130)

which implies that

\displaystyle b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})\,.

(131)

We now consider the choices of
(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B})
where neither of the above two conditions is satisfied.

The case where
\sigma_{\vec{v}}=\sigma_{A}
or
\sigma_{\vec{v}}=\sigma_{B}
:
First, note that if
\sigma_{\vec{v}}=\sigma_{B}
, and Condition 1. is not satisfied, then Eq. (
123
) implies that
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}=\sigma_{A}
and
\gamma=\pm 1
, so Condition 2. is not satisfied either. In this case, we can take another
n
-qubit Pauli operator
\sigma_{C}\notin\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
(which implies that
\sigma_{C}\neq\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B}
), such that
\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}=\pm i\sigma_{BAC}
for some
n
-qubit Pauli
\sigma_{BAC}
. The existence of such a
\sigma_{C}
is guaranteed by:

•

the fact that half of the total
4^{n}
number of
n
-qubit Pauli operators, when multiplied after
\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}
(which is not equal to the identity because
\sigma_{B}\neq\sigma_{A}
by construction), gives a Pauli operator times
\pm 1
, and the other half will give a Pauli operator times
\pm i
,

•

the fact that the set
\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
contains the operators
\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B}
, which, when multiplied after
\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}
, gives a Pauli operator times
\pm 1
,

•

the assumption that
M<4^{n}/2+2
.

Applying the procedure in Eqs. (
120
)–(
131
) above to the unitary
U^{\prime}\coloneqq(\sigma_{C}+\beta^{\prime}\sigma_{B})/\sqrt{2}
(with
\beta^{\prime}\in\{1,i\}
), we find that there is no
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
such that
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime}}\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{C}\sigma_{B}=\sigma_{B}\sigma_{C}\sigma_{B}\propto\sigma_{C}
.
Therefore, Condition 1. is satisfied for
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{C},\sigma_{B})
and we have that

b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{C})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U^{\prime})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})\,.

(132)

Applying the procedure in Eqs. (
120
)–(
131
) above to the unitary
U^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq(\sigma_{A}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\sigma_{C})/\sqrt{2}
(with
\beta^{\prime\prime}\in\{1,i\}
), we find that either (a) there is no
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
such that
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}\propto\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}
, or (b) if there is, then
\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}=\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}=\pm i\sigma_{BAC}
, i.e.
\sigma_{\vec{v}^{\prime\prime}}=\sigma_{BAC}
. Therefore, for
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{C})
, either Condition 1. or 2. is satisfied and we have that

b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U^{\prime\prime})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{C})\,.

(133)

Overall, Eq. (
131
) is satisfied for
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B})
. An analogous argument applies if
\sigma_{\vec{v}}=\sigma_{A}
.

The case where
\sigma_{\vec{v}}\neq\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B}
:

If neither Condition 1. nor Condition 2. are satisfied, then we can take another
n
-qubit Pauli operator

\sigma_{C}\neq\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B}
,
such that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}

\displaystyle=\pm i\sigma_{vAC}\,,

(134)

\displaystyle\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{C}\sigma_{B}

\displaystyle\in\{\pm i\sigma_{vCB}\}\iff\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{C}\in\{\pm i\sigma_{vCB}\}\,,

(135)

for some
n
-qubit Pauli operators
\sigma_{vAC},\sigma_{vCB}
.
The existence of such a
\sigma_{C}
is guaranteed by:

•

the fact that for any two different non-identity
n
-qubit Pauli operators
\sigma_{E},\sigma_{F}
, there exists an
n
-qubit Pauli operator
\sigma_{Q}
such that both
\sigma_{E}\sigma_{Q}
and
\sigma_{F}\sigma_{\rm Q}
are equal to
+i
or
-i
times an
n
-qubit Pauli operator,

•

the fact that for any two different non-identity
n
-qubit Pauli operators
\sigma_{E},\sigma_{F}
, there exists an
n
-qubit Pauli operator
\sigma_{R}
such that
\sigma_{E}\sigma_{R}
equals
\pm i
times an
n
-qubit Pauli operator, while
\sigma_{F}\sigma_{R}
equals
\pm 1
times an
n
-qubit Pauli operator.

This enables
\sigma_{C}
to be chosen according to the following strategy:

•

if there are
\sigma_{E},\sigma_{F}
such that
\sigma_{E}=\pm\sigma_{v}\sigma_{A}
and
\sigma_{F}=\pm\sigma_{v}\sigma_{B}
, then pick
\sigma_{C}=\sigma_{Q}
as defined above,

•

if there are
\sigma_{E},\sigma_{F}
such that either
\sigma_{E}=\pm\sigma_{v}\sigma_{A}
and
\sigma_{F}=\pm i\sigma_{v}\sigma_{B}
, or
\sigma_{F}=\pm i\sigma_{v}\sigma_{A}
and
\sigma_{E}=\pm\sigma_{v}\sigma_{B}
, then pick
\sigma_{C}=\sigma_{R}
as defined above,

•

if there are
\sigma_{E},\sigma_{F}
such that
\sigma_{E}=\pm i\sigma_{v}\sigma_{A}
and
\sigma_{F}=\pm i\sigma_{v}\sigma_{B}
, then pick
\sigma_{C}=\sigma_{F}
, in which case
\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{C}=\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}(\pm i\sigma_{v}\sigma_{B})=\mp i\mathbb{1}
and
\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}=\pm i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}=\mp i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}=\mp i\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}=-\sigma_{C}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}=-(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C})^{\dagger}
(where in the third equality we use the assumption that Conditions 1. and 2. are not satisfied, which implies that
\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{B}=\sigma_{B}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{\vec{v}}
), and therefore
\sigma_{\vec{v}}\sigma_{A}\sigma_{C}
must be proportional to
\pm i
times a Pauli.

Applying the procedure in Eqs. (
120
)–(
131
) above to the unitary
U^{\prime}\coloneqq(\sigma_{C}+\beta^{\prime}\sigma_{B})/\sqrt{2}
(with
\beta^{\prime}\in\{1,i\}
), we find that Condition 1. or 2. is satisfied for
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{C},\sigma_{B})
and we have that

b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{C})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U^{\prime})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{B})\,.

(136)

Applying the procedure in Eqs. (
120
)–(
131
) above to the unitary
U^{\prime\prime}\coloneqq(\sigma_{A}+\beta^{\prime\prime}\sigma_{C})/\sqrt{2}
(with
\beta^{\prime\prime}\in\{1,i\}
), we find that Condition 1. or 2. is satisfied for
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{C})
and we have that

b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{A})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},U^{\prime\prime})=b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{C})\,.

(137)

Overall, Eq. (
131
) is satisfied for
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B})
.

Having shown that for all
\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\}
, for every tuple
(\sigma_{\vec{v}}\in\{\sigma_{\vec{r}_{1}},\ldots,\sigma_{\vec{r}_{M}}\},\sigma_{A},\sigma_{B})
, Eq. (
131
) is satisfied, we conclude that
b(\sigma_{\vec{v}},\sigma_{\vec{q_{1}}})\coloneqq\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}
is independent of the choice of
\sigma_{\vec{q_{1}}}
. This means that

\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}[V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}^{*}}]\,,

(138)

for any
n
-qubit Pauli operator
\sigma_{\vec{q}^{*}}
.

Proving that the redefinition of Eqs. (
109
) and (
110
) satisfies Eq. (
103
)

Equations (
118
) and Eq. (
138
) together show that for all
\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M},\vec{q_{1}}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}
,

\displaystyle|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}^{\prime}O_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{k1}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{v}\in\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{v}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{v}\in\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}[V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}^{*}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{v}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\vec{v}\in\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}}\xi^{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}_{m1}[U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{v}^{*}},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}^{*}}]|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{v}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle
