\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\tilde{\xi}_{k1}(\{U_{i}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{M},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}})|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle\,,

(139)

where
\mathbb{F}_{\vec{v}}\coloneqq\{1\leq i\leq M\mid\vec{r}_{i}=\vec{v}\}
,
\vec{v}^{*}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}
is an arbitrary vector outside of the set
\{\vec{r}_{1},\ldots,\vec{r}_{M}\}\backslash\{\vec{v}\}
, and
\vec{q}^{*}\in\{0,1,2,3\}^{\times n}
is an arbitrary fixed vector.
Therefore,
\tilde{\xi}_{k1}
as defined in Eq. (
109
) indeed satisfies Eq. (
103
).

This also implies that

\displaystyle|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}|\sum_{\vec{r}_{i}}\alpha^{i}_{\vec{r}_{i}}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}*|\sum_{\vec{q}_{1}}\beta^{1}_{\vec{q}_{1}}\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}=\sum_{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha^{j}_{\vec{r}_{j}}\right)\beta_{\vec{q}_{1}}^{1}|C\rangle\!\rangle*\bigotimes_{i=1}^{M}|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{i}O_{i}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l},\vec{q}_{1}}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{M}\alpha^{j}_{\vec{r}_{j}}\right)\beta_{\vec{q}_{1}}^{1}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{M}\tilde{\xi}_{k1}(\{U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\}_{m},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}})|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\left[\sum_{\{\vec{r}_{l}\}_{l\neq k}}\left(\prod_{j=1|j\neq k}^{M}\alpha_{\vec{r}_{j}}^{j}\right)\tilde{\xi}_{k1}(\{U_{m}=\sigma_{\vec{r}_{m}}\}_{m},V_{1}=\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}})\right]\sum_{\vec{r}_{k},\vec{q}_{1}}\alpha^{k}_{\vec{r}_{k}}\beta^{1}_{\vec{q}_{1}}|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}*|\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{1}^{\prime}O_{1}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\tilde{\xi}_{k1}\left(\left\{U_{i}=\sum_{\vec{r}_{i}}{\alpha_{\vec{r}_{i}}^{i}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{i}}}\right\},\ V_{1}=\sum_{\vec{q}_{1}}\beta_{\vec{q}_{1}}^{1}\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\right)|S\rangle\!\rangle*|\sum_{\vec{r}_{k}}\alpha_{\vec{r}_{k}}^{k}\sigma_{\vec{r}_{k}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I_{k}O_{k}}*|\sum_{\vec{q}_{1}}\beta^{1}_{\vec{q}_{1}}\sigma_{\vec{q}_{1}}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{1}O^{\prime}_{1}}\,,

(140)

i.e., Eq. (
110
) also satisfies Eq. (
103
).
∎

C5
Lemma
5
(for
\max\{M,N\}\leq\max\{2,d-1\}
)

Before proving Lemma
5
, we present the definition of a QC-CC higher-order transformation with an arbitrary number of slots
M+N

[
27
]
.

An operator
C\in\mathcal{L}(P\otimes I_{1}\otimes O_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes I_{M+N}\otimes O_{M+N}\otimes F)
corresponds to the Choi operator of a
(M+N)
-slot QC-CC higher-order transformation if it satisfies

\displaystyle C=\sum_{\vec{r}_{M+N}\in\mathrm{Perm}(1,\ldots,M+N)}C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F},

(141)

\displaystyle\textup{such that}\quad\quad C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}\geq 0\quad\forall\ \vec{r}_{M+N},

(142)

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{F}[C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}]=C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{M+N}}}\quad\forall\ \vec{r}_{M+N},

(143)

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{m+1}}\text{tr}_{I_{r_{m+1}}}[C_{P\vec{r}_{m}r_{m+1}}]=C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}\quad\forall\ m\in\{1,\ldots,M+N-1\},\forall\ \vec{r}_{m}\coloneqq(r_{1},\ldots,r_{m}),

(144)

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{1}}\mathrm{tr}_{I_{r_{1}}}[C_{Pr_{1}}]=\mathbb{1}^{P},

(145)

where
\vec{r}_{m}r_{m+1}
represents a vector
(r_{1},\ldots,r_{m},r_{m+1})
, with each vector
\vec{r}_{m}
composed of elements
r_{1},\ldots,r_{m}
, and
the operators
C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}\in\mathcal{L}(P\otimes I_{1}\otimes O_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes I_{m-1}\otimes O_{m-1}\otimes I_{m})
for
m\in\{1,\ldots,M+N\}
are recursively defined by

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{1\over d}\text{tr}_{O_{r_{M+N}}F}[C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}],

(146)

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{1\over d}\sum_{r_{m+1}}\text{tr}_{O_{r_{m}}I_{r_{m+1}}}[C_{P\vec{r}_{m}r_{m+1}}]\quad\forall\ m\in\{1,\ldots,M+N-1\}.

(147)

These conditions will be referred as the
QC-CC conditions
. We remark that quantum combs are a subset of QC-CCs.

For convenience in the following, we rename the last
N
input and output systems as

\displaystyle I^{\prime}_{k}\coloneqq I_{M+k},\quad O^{\prime}_{k}\coloneqq O_{M+k}\quad\forall\ k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}.

(148)

Lemma 5
.

Let
|S\rangle\!\rangle\in P\otimes I\otimes O\otimes I^{\prime}\otimes O^{\prime}\otimes F
, where
I,O,I^{\prime},O^{\prime}
correspond to
d
-dimensional Hilbert spaces and
P,F
correspond to
(2\times d)
-dimensional Hilbert spaces, be the Choi vector of the quantum switch and let
C\in\mathcal{L}(P\otimes I_{1}\otimes O_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes I_{M}\otimes O_{M}\otimes I^{\prime}_{1}\otimes O^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes I^{\prime}_{N}\otimes O^{\prime}_{N}\otimes F)
, where
M,N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
and
\{I_{i}\}_{i},\{O_{i}\}_{i},\{I^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j},\{O^{\prime}_{j}\}_{j}
correspond to
d
-dimensional Hilbert spaces, be the Choi operator of an
(M+N)
-slot QC-CC higher-order transformation, which we write as

\displaystyle C=\sum_{\vec{r}_{M+N}}C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}\,,

(149)

with
\vec{r}_{M+N}\in\mathrm{Perm}(1,\ldots,M+N)
, where all
C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}\geq 0
and, hence, can be decomposed as

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}=\sum_{a}|{C^{(a)}_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}}|\,.

(150)

If
\max(M,N)\leq\max(2,d-1)
, then the set of operators
|{C^{(a)}_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{C^{(a)}_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}}|
cannot be such that

\displaystyle|C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}^{(a)}\rangle\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{k=1}^{N}|S\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{i}O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}O^{\prime}_{k}F}\otimes|\tilde{\xi}_{ik}^{(a),\vec{r}_{M+N}}\rangle\!\rangle,

(151)

for all
\vec{r}_{M+N}
and
a
, where
|\tilde{\xi}_{ik}^{(a),\vec{r}_{M+N}}\rangle\!\rangle\in I_{\bar{i}}\otimes O_{\bar{i}}\otimes I^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}\otimes O^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
and
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, and
I_{\bar{i}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i^{\prime}\neq i}I_{i^{\prime}}
,
O_{\bar{i}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{i^{\prime}\neq i}O_{i^{\prime}}
,
I^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k^{\prime}\neq k}I^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
, and
O^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}\coloneqq\bigotimes_{k^{\prime}\neq k}O^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

The proof follows by contradiction. To this end, we use Eqs. (
143
) and (
144
) in the QC-CC conditions to show the following equation for
C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{r_{m}}\text{tr}_{I_{r_{m}}}[C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}]&=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}}\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}}\Big(\outerproduct{0}{0}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\\
&+\outerproduct{1}{1}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\Big)\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m-1}}^{(ijkl)}\quad\forall m\in\{1,\ldots,M+N+1\},\end{split}

(152)

where the summation over
r_{m}
for
m=M+N+1
is taken as
I_{r_{M+N+1}}\coloneqq F
,
C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N+1}}
is defined by
C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N+1}}\coloneqq C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}
, the set of indices
\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{m-1}\}\cap\{1,\ldots,M\},

(153)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{r_{1}-M,\ldots,r_{m-1}-M\}\cap\{1,\ldots,N\},

(154)

and
C_{P\vec{r}_{m-1}}^{(ijkl)}
is an operator.
If this equation holds, since
\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{0}}
and
\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{0}}
are the empty sets, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{r_{1}}\text{tr}_{I_{r_{1}}}[C_{Pr_{1}}]=0,

(155)

which contradicts with the normalization condition in Eq. (
145
) of the QC-CC conditions.
In the rest of the proof, we show Eq. (
152
) by induction with respect to
m
.

First, we show Eq. (
152
) for
m=M+N+1
as follows.
Since the operator
C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}
can be written as

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,l}|S\rangle\!\rangle^{PI_{i}O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}O^{\prime}_{k}F}\langle\!\langle S|^{PI_{j}O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}O^{\prime}_{l}F}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}}^{(ijkl)},

(156)

where
C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}}^{(ijkl)}\coloneqq\sum_{a}|{\tilde{\xi}_{ik}^{(a),\vec{r}_{M+N}}}\rangle\!\rangle\!\langle\!\langle{\tilde{\xi}_{jl}^{(a),\vec{r}_{M+N}}}|
.
The partial trace
\text{tr}_{F}C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{tr}_{F}[C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}F}]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{M}\sum_{k,l=1}^{N}\Big(&\outerproduct{0}{0}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\\
+&\outerproduct{1}{1}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\Big)\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{M+N}}^{(ijkl)}\,,\end{split}

(157)

i.e., Eq. (
152
) holds for
m=M+N+1
.

To complete the proof, we show Eq. (
152
) by assuming Eq. (
152
) for
m\leftarrow m+1
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{r_{m+1}}\text{tr}_{I_{r_{m+1}}}[C_{P\vec{r}_{m}r_{m+1}}]=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\Big(&\outerproduct{0}{0}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\\
+&\outerproduct{1}{1}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\Big)\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}.\end{split}

(158)

By symmetry with
(I_{i},O_{i})
and
(I^{\prime}_{k},O^{\prime}_{k})
, it is sufficient to show if
r_{m}\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
holds.
From Eq. (
144
) [or Eq. (
143
) for
m=M+N
] in the QC-CC conditions and Eq. (
158
), we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\Big(&\outerproduct{0}{0}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\\
+&\outerproduct{1}{1}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\Big)\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\\
=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}&\outerproduct{0}{0}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes A_{ijkl}\\
+&\outerproduct{1}{1}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes B_{ijkl},\end{split}

(159)

where
A_{ijkl}
and
B_{ijkl}
are defined by

\displaystyle A_{ijkl}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i,j\neq r_{m})\\
{1\over d}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{k}O_{r_{m}}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i=r_{m}\neq j)\\
{1\over d}|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{I^{\prime}_{k}O_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{l}}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(j=r_{m}\neq i)\\
{1\over d}\mathbb{1}^{I^{\prime}_{l}\to I^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i=j=r_{m})\end{cases},

(160)

\displaystyle B_{ijkl}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\otimes\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i,j\neq r_{m})\\
{1\over d}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{r_{m}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i=r_{m}\neq j)\\
{1\over d}\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}\to O_{i}}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(j=r_{m}\neq i)\\
C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i=j=r_{m})\end{cases},

(161)

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{1\over d}\text{tr}_{O_{r_{m}}}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}.

(162)

Using Lemma
6
for Eq. (
159
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes A_{ijkl}\quad\forall i,j,

(163)

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}

\displaystyle=B_{ijkl}\quad\forall i,j,k,l.

(164)

From Eq. (
164
), we obtain

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i,j\neq r_{m})\\
0&(i=r_{m}\neq j\;\mathrm{or}\;j=r_{m}\neq i)\end{cases},

(165)

where the cases of
i=r_{m}\neq j
and
j=r_{m}\neq i
are shown as below.
If
i=r_{m}\neq j
holds, from Eqs. (
161
) and (
164
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{r_{m}}}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}={1\over d}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{r_{m}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}.

(166)

By taking the inner product of Eq. (
166
) with
\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{r_{m}}}
, we obtain

\displaystyle dC_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}={1\over d}C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)},

(167)

i.e.,
C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}=0
holds for
i=r_{m}\neq j
.
We can similarly show that
C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}=0
for
j=r_{m}\neq i
.
From Eq. (
163
) for
i=j=r_{m}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}=\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m}}}\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{I^{\prime}_{l}\to I^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes{\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}\over d}\otimes C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\quad\mathrm{if}\;i=j=r_{m}.

(168)

Using Lemma
7
, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}=0\quad\mathrm{if}\;i=j=r_{m}.

(169)

In conclusion, we obtain

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}&(i,j\neq r_{m})\\
0&(\mathrm{otherwise})\end{cases}.

(170)

Thus, from Eqs. (
144
) and (
158
), we obtain

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{m}}=\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{A}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}}\sum_{k,l\in\mathbb{B}_{\vec{r}_{m-1}}}\Big(

\displaystyle\outerproduct{0}{0}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{i}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{j}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}

(171)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\outerproduct{1}{1}^{P_{C}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O_{j}\to O_{i}}\Big)\otimes\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}.

(172)

Thus, defining
C_{P\vec{r}_{m-1}}^{(ijkl)}
by

\displaystyle C_{P\vec{r}_{m-1}}^{(ijkl)}\coloneqq\sum_{r_{m}}\text{tr}_{I_{r_{m}}}[\tilde{C}_{P\vec{r}_{m}}^{(ijkl)}],

(173)

we obtain Eq. (
152
). We finish the proof by recalling that Eq. (
152
) implies Eq. (
155
), which contradicts the normalization condition in Eq. (
145
) of the QC-CC conditions.
∎

C6
Lemma
6

Lemma 6
.

The set of matrices

\displaystyle\left\{|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{k}^{\prime}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{l}^{\prime}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I_{\bar{k}}^{\prime}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I_{\bar{l}}^{\prime}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\gamma}}^{I_{\bar{i}}}\bra{\vec{\delta}}^{I_{\bar{j}}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in\{1,\ldots,M\},k,l\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\\
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{N-1},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{M-1}\end{subarray}}

(174)

is linearly independent if
\max(M,N)\leq d
holds. Similarly, the set of matrices

\displaystyle\left\{|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{j}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I_{\bar{i}}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I_{\bar{j}}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in\{1,\ldots,M\},\\
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{M-1}\end{subarray}}

(175)

is linearly independent if
M\leq d
holds.

Proof.

We consider the equation

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j,k,l,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}}A_{ijkl\vec{\alpha}\vec{\beta}\vec{\gamma}\vec{\delta}}|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{P_{T}I_{k}^{\prime}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{P_{T}I_{l}^{\prime}}\otimes|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I_{\bar{k}}^{\prime}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I_{\bar{l}}^{\prime}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\gamma}}^{I_{\bar{i}}}\bra{\vec{\delta}}^{I_{\bar{j}}}=0

(176)

for complex coefficients
A_{ijkl\vec{\alpha}\vec{\beta}\vec{\gamma}\vec{\delta}}
.
Since
\max(M,N)\leq d
holds, for all
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}
, there exists
\alpha^{*},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
such that
\alpha^{*},\beta^{*},\gamma^{*},\delta^{*}
do not appear in
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}
, respectively.
By taking an inner product of Eq. (
176
) with
\ket{\alpha^* \alpha^*}^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{k}}\bra{\beta^* \beta^*}^{P_{T}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\ket{\gamma^* \gamma^*}^{O^{\prime}_{k}I_{i}}\bra{\delta^* \delta^*}^{O^{\prime}_{l}I_{j}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I_{\bar{k}}^{\prime}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I_{\bar{l}}^{\prime}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\gamma}}^{I_{\bar{i}}}\bra{\vec{\delta}}^{I_{\bar{j}}}
for any
i,j,k,l,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}
, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{ijkl\vec{\alpha}\vec{\beta}\vec{\gamma}\vec{\delta}}=0,

(177)

i.e., the set (
174
) is linearly independent.
We can similarly show that the set (
175
) is linearly independent.
∎

C7
Lemma
7

Lemma 7
.

The set of matrices

\displaystyle\left\{\left(|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{l}}-{\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}\over d}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{I^{\prime}_{l}\to I^{\prime}_{k}}\right)\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I^{\prime}_{\bar{l}}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\gamma}}^{O^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}}\bra{\vec{\delta}}^{O^{\prime}_{\bar{l}}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\in\{1,\ldots,N\},\\
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}^{N-1}\end{subarray}}

(178)

is linearly independent if
N\leq\max(2,d-1)
holds.

Proof.

We numerically check the linear independence for the case
N=d=2
(see Listing
).
We prove the linear independence for the case
N\leq d-1
to complete the proof.

We consider the equation

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}}A_{kl\vec{\alpha}\vec{\beta}\vec{\gamma}\vec{\delta}}\left(|\mathbb{1}\rangle\!\rangle^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{k}}\langle\!\langle\mathbb{1}|^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{l}}-{\mathbb{1}^{O_{r_{m}}}\over d}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{I^{\prime}_{l}\to I^{\prime}_{k}}\right)\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I^{\prime}_{\bar{l}}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\gamma}}^{O^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}}\bra{\vec{\delta}}^{O^{\prime}_{\bar{l}}}=0

(179)

for complex coefficients
A_{kl\vec{\alpha}\vec{\beta}\vec{\gamma}\vec{\delta}}
.
Since
N\leq d-1
holds, for all
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta}
, there exists
\alpha^{*},\beta^{*}\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
such that
\alpha^{*}\neq\beta^{*}
holds and
\alpha^{*},\beta^{*}
do not appear in
\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta}
, respectively.
By taking an inner product of Eq. (
179
) with
{1\over d}\ket{\alpha^*\alpha^*}^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{k}}\bra{\beta^* \beta^*}^{O_{r_{m}}I^{\prime}_{l}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\alpha}}^{I^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}}\bra{\vec{\beta}}^{I^{\prime}_{\bar{l}}}\otimes\mathbb{1}^{O^{\prime}_{l}\to O^{\prime}_{k}}\otimes\ket{\vec{\gamma}}^{O^{\prime}_{\bar{k}}}\bra{\vec{\delta}}^{O^{\prime}_{\bar{l}}}
for any
k,l,\vec{\alpha},\vec{\beta},\vec{\gamma},\vec{\delta}
, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{kl\vec{\alpha}\vec{\beta}\vec{\gamma}\vec{\delta}}=0,

(180)

i.e., the set (
178
) is linearly independent.
∎

Appendix D
Possible restricted simulations for qubit channels

From this section onwards, we revert to our original notation.

Using numerical methods, we have found three particular cases where a restricted simulation of the quantum switch acting on qubit channels
is possible
, exactly and deterministically, using a quantum comb. These are the cases of:

•

Four identical calls to general qubit channels, called the order AAAA.

•

Two calls to unitary qubit channels
A
and two calls to unitary qubit channels
B
, in the order AABB.

•

Three calls to unitary qubit channels
A
and one call to unitary qubit channels
B
, in the order BAAA.

All of these results were obtained by numerically evaluating the equivalent of the primal SDP of the main text [Eq. (
27
)] in the case where the input states are fixed and the output target system is discarded, and finding that
p=1
up to a high numerical precision.

Effectively, this scenario amounts to an SDP that is analogous to the primal SDP (
27
) in the main text, but with the first constraint written as

C_{s}*\Big[{(J^{A}_{i})}^{\otimes k_{A}}\otimes{(J^{B}_{j})}^{\otimes k_{B}}\Big]=p\,\text{tr}_{t_{O}}(S_{+0})*(J^{A}_{i}\otimes J^{B}_{j})\ \ \ \forall\,i,j,

(181)

where
S_{+0}
is defined as in Eq. (
36
). In this case, we have that
C_{s},C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}})
and
d_{c_{I}}=d_{t_{I}}=1
.

In the case of
4
identical copies of general qubit channels, the input channels were given as a basis constructed in the form of Eq. (
33
) of the main text. The dimension of the space spanned by
k=4
identical copies of a general qubit channel is
d_{\mathcal{I}}=1820
, a value that can be obtained from the expression presented in the Methods section of the main text.

In the case of
k
identical copies of qubit unitary channels, a basis can be constructed numerically by randomly sampling a set of qubit unitaries according to the Haar measure, guaranteeing that they are linearly independent. It is only necessary to know the dimension of this subspace beforehand to determine how many unitaries must be sampled. The dimension of the linear space spanned by
k
copies of
d
-dimension unitary channels is the quantity
D(d,r,s)
of Ref.
[
65
]
for the case
r=s=k
. For
d=2
, the dimension of the subspace spanned by
k
identical copies of a unitary channel is given by
d_{\mathcal{U}}=D(d=2,k,k)=\binom{2k+3}{3}
, which implies
d_{\mathcal{U}}=10
for
k=1
,
d_{\mathcal{U}}=35
for
k=2
, and
d_{\mathcal{U}}=84
for
k=3
.

By numerically evaluating the appropriate SDP, we obtain that the maximum probability of success is
p=1
with very high precision in all aforementioned cases. The numerical precision was evaluated in the following way: In all three cases, all inequality constrains are strictly satisfied; as for the equality constraints, they are satisfied up to an error of at most
10^{-9}
in the operators norm for the case of identical general qubit channels AAAA, and at most
10^{-7}
in the cases of qubit unitary channels AABB and BAAA.

We also found that the maximum probability of success remains
p=1
, with the same precision, in all three cases when the second and third slots of the quantum comb
\mathcal{C}
are parallelized, as depicted in Fig.
D1
.

Figure D1:
Semi-parallelized strategy for restricted qubit simulations.
The quantum switch acting on qubit channels can be deterministically simulated by a
4
-slot quantum comb that has its second and third slots parallelized in a restricted scenario where the input control and target systems are fixed and the output target system is discarded in three different cases: for identical general quantum channels (i.e. when
A=B
) called order AAAA, and for unitary channels (i.e. when
A=U_{A}
and
B=U_{B}
) in the orders AABB and BAAA.

Impossible partly restricted simulation.

As mentioned in the main text, we also found that when the output target system is not discarded—i.e., the partly restricted simulation scenario—then a deterministic simulation is no longer possible in all 3 aforementioned cases.

More concretely, this is the scenario where the first constraint in the primal SDP (
27
) in the main text is written as

C_{s}*\Big[{(J^{A}_{i})}^{\otimes k_{A}}\otimes{(J^{B}_{j})}^{\otimes k_{B}}\Big]=p\,S_{+0}*(J^{A}_{i}\otimes J^{B}_{j})\ \ \ \forall\,i,j,

(182)

where
S_{+0}
is defined as in Eq. (
36
),
C_{s},C\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H}^{A_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{A_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B_{I}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{B_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{t_{O}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{c_{O}})
, and
d_{c_{I}}=d_{t_{I}}=1
.

Evaluating the above SDP, for the partly restricted scenario, we found that
p<1
in the AAAA case of identical general qubit channels, the AABB case of qubit unitary channels, as well as the BAAA case of qubit unitary channels. Due to limitations in computational power, the partly restricted simulation SDP was evaluated in all three cases with an input that corresponds to a subset of the basis that was given as input to the restricted simulation SDP. Therefore, the solutions for the maximal probability of success correspond to upper bounds, and are given by

order = AAAA (qubit, general),
.

\displaystyle p\text{{ $\lesssim$ }}0.942

(183)

\displaystyle\text{order = AABB (qubit, unitary)},\ \ \

\displaystyle p\text{{ $\lesssim$ }}0.822

(184)

\displaystyle\text{order = BAAA (qubit, unitary)},\ \ \

\displaystyle p\text{{ $\lesssim$ }}0.667.

(185)

The difference between the two simulation scenarios discussed in this section is whether or not the output target state is discarded. Here we can see that this distinction in the requirements of the simulation is sufficient to transform cases where a deterministic simulation is possible (with a discarded output target system) into cases where a deterministic simulation is no longer possible (without discarding the output target system).

Appendix E
No-go results for unitary channels

As previously discussed, the simulation of the action of the quantum switch on unitary channels is possible when an extra call of one of the input channels is available. This was first shown by Ref.
[
6
]
for the action of the quantum switch on entire single-party unitary channels, and here we have shown this result to extend to a more general scenario where the quantum switch acts only on part of the input bipartite unitary channels (see Theorem
1
in Sec.
B4
). These results hold in the general simulation scenario, where the input systems are not fixed and the output systems are not discarded, and hold as well for any dimension.

However, this result crucially depends not only on the number of extra calls available, but also on the order in which the quantum unitary channels are applied in the simulation. The cases where a simulation is possible for either single-party or for bipartite unitary channels with
(k_{A},k_{B})=(2,1)
use the order ABA. If the order of the applied channels is instead either AAB or BAA, we find that a deterministic simulation is no longer possible, even in the restricted qubit scenario.

Moreover, the existence of a simulation for unitary channels in the order ABA implies that, when
(k_{A},k_{B})=(2,2)
, a simulation with the orders ABAB and ABBA also trivially exists. In Sec.
D
, we showed that a simulation using the order AABB also exists for unitary channels, albeit
only
in the restricted qubit case where the output target system is discarded. For the case where
(k_{A},k_{B})=(3,1)
, a simulation in the orders AABA and ABAA exists, following trivially from the result for ABA. We showed in Sec.
D
that a simulation for unitary channels in the order BAAA exists as well, but once again
only
in the restricted qubit case. The one case left to study then is a simulation of the quantum switch acting on unitary channels in the order AAAB. We show such a simulation is not possible.

Hence, our three no-go results for simulations of the quantum switch acting exclusively on unitary channels concern the cases of:

•

Two calls of the unitary channels
A
and one call of unitary channels
B
, in the order AAB.

•

Two calls of the unitary channels
A
and one call of unitary channels
B
, in the order BAA.

•

Three calls of the unitary channels
A
and one call of unitary channels
B
, in the order AAAB.

In order to show these results, we numerically evaluate the maximum probability of success of simulating the quantum switch when the input channels are unitary. We do so in the case where the input channels are acting on qubit systems, i.e., when
d=2
, and in the restricted simulation scenario where the input control and target systems are fixed, and the output target system is discarded [see Eq. (
181
)]. We remark once again that the impossibility of a deterministic simulation in the restricted, fixed-dimension case implies the impossibility of a deterministic simulation in general.

In this case, we do not construct an explicit basis analytically for the subspace spanned by
k
identical copies of a qubit unitary channel, but instead, we randomly sample a set of
d_{\mathcal{I}}
linearly independent qubit unitary channels
\{U_{i}^{\otimes k}\}_{i=1}^{d_{\mathcal{I}}}
to form our basis. We repeat here that for the case where
k=1
,
d_{\mathcal{U}}=10
, for
k=2
,
d_{\mathcal{U}}=35
, and for
k=3
, and
d_{\mathcal{U}}=84
. This implies that in the
(k_{A},k_{B})=(2,1)
case, one needs
35\cdot 10=350
pairs of qubit unitary channels and for
(k_{A},k_{B})=(3,1)
, one needs
84\cdot 10=840
pairs of qubit unitary channels as input for the primal and dual SDPs [Eqs. (
27
) and (
28
) of the main text]. We numerically obtain the values of

\displaystyle\text{order = AAB\phantom{A} (qubit, unitary)},\ \ \

\displaystyle p\approx 0.600

(186)

\displaystyle\text{order = BAA\phantom{A} (qubit, unitary)},\ \ \

\displaystyle p\approx 0.851

(187)

\displaystyle\text{order = AAAB (qubit, unitary)},\ \ \

\displaystyle p\approx 0.708.

(188)

Appendix F
Efficient certification that a matrix is positive semidefinite

A self-adjoint linear operator
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
is positive semidefinite if and only if there exists an operator
L\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{C}^{d})
such that
A=LL^{\dagger}
. Moreover, the operator
L
can be taken to be a lower triangular matrix, i.e., a matrix in which all entries above the main diagonal are zero. The decomposition of a positive semidefinite matrix as
A=LL^{\dagger}
is referred to as the Cholesky decomposition, and finding such a decomposition can be done efficiently
[
66
]
.

In the final step of the algorithm used for computer-assisted proofs presented in the Methods section of the main text, one is required to certify that an operator
A
is positive semidefinite. When
A
is stored as a symbolic matrix, due to the way computers manipulate symbolic variables, if the matrix
A
is not sparse enough, ensuring that
A\geq 0
may be a prohibitively time-consuming task even when using the Cholesky decomposition. This was the case, for instance, when trying to ensure that the matrix
\overline{\mathbb{P}}(\Gamma^{\texttt{sym}})+\eta\mathbb{1}-\sum_{i,j}R_{ij}^{\texttt{OK}}\otimes({J^{A}_{i}}^{\otimes k_{A}}\otimes{J^{B}_{j}}^{\otimes k_{B}})^{T}
in our computer-assisted proofs algorithm involving
k_{A}+k_{B}=4
-slot quantum combs. Below, we describe an algorithm that can be used to ensure that a symbolic matrix
A
is positive semidefinite, which is considerably faster than performing Cholesky decomposition on a symbolic matrix
A
.

The algorithm we present below is based on three key ideas.

1.

It is possible to rigorously certify that a matrix is positive definite using floating-point arithmetic quickly. One way to attain this goal is to use the methods presented in Ref.
[
67
]
. It shows how to efficiently certify that a matrix
A
is positive definite using a rigorous algorithm that accounts for all possible computational and rounding errors and remains valid in the presence of underflow.

2.

If a matrix
A
is “close” to another matrix
A^{\prime}
, and
A^{\prime}
is “far” from the set of non-positive semidefinite matrices, then
A
has to be positive semidefinite.

3.

If
A
is matrix with symbolic entries, we can obtain a floating-point variable matrix
A^{\prime}
that is guaranteed to be close to
A
. This can be done via arbitrary-precision arithmetic
[
68
]
.

Algorithm to prove that a symbolic self-adjoint matrix
A
is positive semidefinite:

1.

Construct a self-adjoint matrix
A^{\prime}
that is equal to
A
up to
n
decimal digits

This step can be accomplished using arbitrary-precision arithmetic
[
68
]
with a precision of
n
decimal digits. In this way, all matrix elements of
A^{\prime}-A
are between
-10^{-n+1}
and
10^{-n+1}
, ensuring that

\displaystyle-J\cdot 10^{-n+1}\;\leq\;A^{\prime}-A\;\leq\;J\cdot 10^{-n+1},

(189)

where
J\geq 0
is a matrix in which all entries are the number one.

2.

Prove that
A^{\prime}-J\geq 0
using the algorithm presented in Ref.
[
67
]
.

Since
A^{\prime}-J\cdot 10^{-n+1}\leq A
holds, if we ensure that
A^{\prime}-J\geq 0
, then by transitivity it follows that
A\geq 0
.
